Positivity and Web Bases for Specht Modules of Hecke Algebras Thanks: Research of the first author was partially supported by the McNair Scholars program and the Oklahoma Louis Stokes Alliances for Minority Participation (OK–LSAMP) program. Research of the second author was partially supported by a Simons Collaboration Grant.
Samuel David Heard, Jonathan R. Kujawa

Abstract
. We show that the transition matrix from the standard basis to the web basis for a Specht module of the Hecke algebra is unitriangular and satisfies a strong positivity property whenever the Specht module is labeled by a partition with at most two parts. This generalizes results of Russell–Tymoczko and Rhoades.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 20C08, 05E10; Secondary 20C30

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background

The Specht module
S^{(n,n)}
is a simple module for the symmetric group
S_{2n}
defined over the rational numbers. It admits (at least) two interesting combinatorial bases. The first is the
polytabloid basis
indexed by the standard tableaux of shape
(n,n)
. Tableaux combinatorics play an important role not only in the representation theory of the symmetric group, but also symmetric functions, Schubert and Springer varieties, and more (e.g., see
[
7
]
). The second is the
web basis
given by non-crossing pairings of
\{1,\dotsc,2n\}
. Webs arise in the diagrammatic description of certain categories of representations of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
and related algebras, are connected to various well-studied diagrammatic algebras (e.g., Temperley–Lieb and Brauer algebras), and appear in various guises in algebraic combinatorics, low-dimensional topology, cluster algebras, invariant theory, and categorical representation theory (e.g., see
[
5
,
6
,
14
,
19
,
22
]
and related references).

In
[
24
]
, Russell and Tymoczko investigated the relationship between these two bases of
S^{(n,n)}
. They showed that under a suitable ordering the change of basis matrix is unitriangular. In this context the web basis is known to coincide with the Kazhdan–Lusztig basis and, as such, unitriangularity can be deduced from the work of Garsia–McLarnan
[
8
]
or Naruse
[
17
]
. Russell–Tymoczko gave a more direct, combinatorial proof of this result. They also sharpened it by giving additional conditions on when an entry can be nonzero. Moreover, based on their results and the available data, Russell–Tymoczko conjectured that the entries of this matrix are always nonnegative. Rhoades gave a proof of the Russell–Tymoczko positivity conjecture in
[
21
]
. This positivity property is known to be false for Specht modules in general and it remains an open question to determine when it holds.

1.2.
Main results

The purpose of the present paper is to generalize this work in two directions. First, we replace the group algebra
\mathbb{Q}S_{d}
with the Hecke algebra
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
defined over
\mathbb{Q}(q)
. Second, we consider analogues of the polytabloid and web bases of the Specht module
S^{\lambda}
for
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
where
\lambda=(a,b)
is an arbitrary two-row partition of
d
.

In this setting the analogue of the polytabloid basis of
S^{\lambda}
is naturally indexed by the set of standard tableaux of whose shape is the transpose of
\lambda
,
\operatorname{Std}(\lambda^{\mathtt{T}})
. The web basis is given by the set of non-crossing pairings of
\{1,\dotsc,a+b,1^{\prime},\dotsc,(a-b)^{\prime}\}
,
\mathcal{W}^{(a,b)}
. We define a combinatorial bijection

\psi:\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)\to\mathcal{W}^{(a,b)}

and partial order
\preceq
on
\mathcal{W}^{(a,b)}
which generalize those given in
[
24
]
. Our main results are
Theorems

5.1.1
,
5.1.3
and
5.2.1
. These unitriangularity and strong positivity results are summarized in the following theorem.

Theorem
.

Let
\lambda=(a,b)
be a partition of
d
with two parts. For every
t\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda^{\mathtt{T}})
,

v_{t}=\psi(t^{\mathtt{T}})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\mathcal{W}^{(a,b)}\\
w\prec\psi(t^{\mathtt{T}})\end{subarray}}c_{w,t}w,

where
c_{w,t}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}[q]
.

We refer the reader to
Section

6.2
and
Section

6.3
for examples when
\lambda=(3,3)
and
\lambda=(4,2)
, respectively.
Setting
q=1
and
a=b
recovers the results of
[
21
,
24
]
.

1.3.
Additional questions

Work of Im–Zhu
[
10
]
and Hwang–Jang–Oh
[
9
]
gives combinatorial interpretations for the entries of this change of basis matrix when
q=1
and
a=b
. In particular, they determine precisely when an entry can be nonzero and thereby verify the conjecture of Russell–Tymoczko that the conditions they gave for non-vanishing were both necessary and sufficient. It would be interesting to refine and generalize these results in order to describe the matrix entries as polynomials in
q
for all two-row partitions. In particular, in the examples we have computed each entry of the matrix is palindromic in that it can be written in the form
q^{t}f
where
f\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}[q,q^{-1}]
and
f(q)=f(q^{-1})
. A combinatorial model for these polynomials could help answer if this behavior always occurs. Related is the work of McDonough–Pallikaros which establishes unitriangularity between the standard basis and the Kazhdan–Lusztig basis for Specht modules of
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
and that the change of basis entries can be described using Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials
[
16
]
.

In a different direction, there are versions of the polytabloid and web/skein bases when the partition has three equal rows,
\lambda=(n,n,n)
, or is of a flag shape,
\lambda=(n,n,1^{k})
. See, for example,
[
18
,
20
,
23
]
. The Tubbenhauer–Vaz–Wedrich diagrams we use here are defined for
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
for all
n\geq 1
and, hence, can be used for partitions with any number of parts. Our methods could be used to investigate these and other shapes.

In a third direction, Khovanov–Mazorchuk–Stroppel gave a categorification of Specht modules via subcategories of graded parabolic category
\mathcal{O}
for
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
where the action of the generators of
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
are given by translation functors
[
13
]
. In this categorification the isomorphism classes of the indecomposable projective-injective modules are in bijection with the Kazhdan–Lusztig basis of the Specht module. We expect the results of this paper can be interpreted as graded multiplicities in this categorification.

2.
Hecke Algebras and Specht Modules

2.1.
Preliminaries

Throughout the base field is
\mathbbm{k}=\mathbb{Q}(q)
, where
q
is an indeterminant. Given a nonnegative integer
k
and a
t\in\mathbbm{k}
which is not a root of unity, let

[k]_{t}=\frac{t^{k}-t^{-k}}{t-t^{-1}}=t^{k-1}+t^{k-3}+\dots+t^{-(k-3)}+t^{-(k-1)}\in\mathbbm{k}.

Set

[k]_{t}!=[k]_{t}[k-1]_{t}\dotsb[2]_{t}[1]_{t}.

A partition
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dotsc)
of
d
is a weakly decreasing sequence of nonnegative integers which sum to
d
. Write
\lambda\vdash d
to indicate that
\lambda
is a partition of
d
. The nonzero entries of
\lambda
are called the parts of
\lambda
. let
\Lambda_{+}(2)
be the set of all partitions with two or fewer parts. For
d\geq 0
, let
\Lambda_{+}(2,d)
be the set of all partitions of
d
with two or fewer parts.

Given a partition
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{n})\vdash d
, let
S_{\lambda}\cong S_{\lambda_{1}}\times\dotsb\times S_{\lambda_{n}}
denote the corresponding Young subgroup of the symmetric group on
d
letters,
S_{d}
. Let
s_{1},\dotsc,s_{d-1}\in S_{d}
denote the simple transpositions and let
\ell:S_{d}\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
be the corresponding length function.

Given a partition,
\lambda
, there is the corresponding Young diagram consisting of
\lambda_{i}
boxes in the
i
th row, left justified. We use the so-called English notation when drawing Young diagrams. A tableau of shape
\lambda
is a filling of the boxes of the Young diagram for
\lambda
with the numbers
1,\dotsc,d
. A standard tableau of shape
\lambda
is a tableau of shape
\lambda
which satisfies the property that the entries strictly increase as one goes left-to-right along rows and top-to-bottom along columns. Given a partition,
\lambda\vdash d
, let
t_{0}=t^{\lambda}
be the distinguished standard tableau given by filling the boxes of
\lambda
with
1,\dotsc,d
in order by filling in each row from left to right, starting with the top row. Let
\operatorname{Tab}(\lambda)
be the set of all tableaux of shape
\lambda
and let
\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
be the set of all standard tableaux of shape
\lambda
.

Given a partition
\lambda
let
\lambda^{\mathtt{T}}
denote the transpose partion. Likewise, given a tableau
t
let
t^{\mathtt{T}}
denote the transpose tableau. There is a right action of
S_{d}
on the set of tableaux of shape
\lambda
by acting on the entries of a tableau. We write
t.\sigma
for this action. The transpose map is
S_{d}
-equivariant. For each partition
\lambda\vdash d
, fix
\sigma_{\lambda}\in S_{d}
once and for all by the equality

(2.1.1)

(t^{\lambda}.\sigma_{\lambda})^{\mathtt{T}}=t^{\lambda^{\mathtt{T}}}.

2.2.
The Hecke algebra of type A

Definition 2.2.1
.

Given a nonnegative integer
d
, let
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
denote the associative, unital
\mathbbm{k}
-algebra generated by
T_{1},\dots,T_{d-1}
subject to the relations (for all admissible
1\leq i,j\leq d-1
):

•

T_{i}T_{j}=T_{j}T_{i}
for
|i-j|>1
;

•

T_{i+1}T_{i}T_{i+1}=T_{i}T_{i+1}T_{i}
;

•

(T_{i}-q)(T_{i}+q^{-1})=0
.

For a reduced expression
w=s_{i_{1}}\dotsb s_{i_{r}}
, set
T_{w}=T_{i_{1}}\dotsb T_{i_{r}}
. This is independent of the choice of reduced expression and the set
\left\{T_{w}\mid w\in S_{d}\right\}
is a
\mathbbm{k}
-basis for
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
.

2.3.
Specht modules

The representation theory of
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
is well-known. We give a brief summary in order to establish conventions. We generally follow
[
3
,
15
]
. Given a partition
\lambda\vdash d
, define the following elements of
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
:

x_{\lambda}=\sum_{w\in S_{\lambda}}q^{\ell(w)}T_{w},\hskip 36.135pty_{\lambda}=\sum_{w\in S_{\lambda}}(-q)^{-\ell(w)}T_{w},

and

z_{\lambda}=x_{\lambda}T_{\sigma_{\lambda}}y_{\lambda^{\mathtt{T}}},

where
\sigma_{\lambda}
is as in
Eq.

2.1.1
.

The
Specht module
associated to
\lambda\vdash d
is the right
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
-module generated by
z_{\lambda}
,

S^{\lambda}=z_{\lambda}\operatorname{H}_{d}(q).

For example, if
\lambda=(d)
, then
x_{\lambda}=\sum_{w\in S_{d}}q^{\ell(w)}T_{w}
,
y_{\lambda^{\mathtt{T}}}=1
,
\sigma_{\lambda}=e_{S_{d}}
, and
S^{\lambda}
is the one-dimensional
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
-module spanned by
z_{\lambda}=x_{\lambda}
which satisfies
z_{\lambda}T_{i}=qz_{\lambda}
for all
i=1,\dotsc,d-1
. If
\lambda=(1,1,\dotsc,1)
, then
x_{\lambda}=1
,
y_{\lambda^{\mathtt{T}}}=\sum_{w\in S_{d}}(-q)^{-\ell(w)}T_{w}
,
\sigma_{\lambda}=e_{S_{d}}
, and
S^{\lambda}
is the one-dimensional
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
-module spanned by
z_{\lambda}=y_{\lambda^{\mathtt{T}}}
which satisfies
z_{\lambda}T_{i}=-q^{-1}z_{\lambda}
for all
i=1,\dotsc,d-1
.

It is known that

\left\{S^{\lambda}\mid\lambda\vdash d\right\}

is a complete, irredundant set of simple
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
-modules. The following lemma is well-known; e.g., see
[
3
, Corollary 4.2]
or
[
15
, Exercise 3.18(iv)]
.

Lemma 2.3.1
.

Given a partition
\lambda\vdash d
,
z_{\lambda}y_{\lambda^{\mathtt{T}}}
is a nonzero scalar multiple of
z_{\lambda}
. Moreover, if
v\in S^{\lambda}
has the property that
vy_{\lambda^{\mathtt{T}}}=cv
for a nonzero scalar
c\in\mathbbm{k}
, then
v\in\mathbbm{k}z_{\lambda}
.

2.4.
The weak Bruhat order

Recall that the
(right) weak Bruhat order
,
\leq
, on
S_{d}
is defined by
u\leq v
if and only if there is a reduced expression
v=s_{i_{1}}\dotsb s_{i_{t}}
and a
0\leq k\leq t
such that
u=s_{i_{1}}\dotsb s_{i_{k}}
. Write
u\leq_{j}v
if there is a reduced expression
v=s_{i_{1}}\dotsb s_{i_{t-1}}s_{i_{t}}
,
u=s_{i_{1}}\dotsb s_{i_{t-1}}
, and
s_{i_{t}}=s_{j}
. Then
u\leq v
if and only if
u=v
or there are sequences
u_{1},\dotsc,u_{r}
and
1\leq j_{1},\dotsc,j_{r}\leq d-1
such that

u\leq_{j_{1}}u_{1}\leq_{j_{2}}\dotsb\leq_{j_{r}}u_{r}=v.

Given a partition
\lambda\vdash d
, define

\mathcal{D}^{st}_{\lambda}=\left\{\tau\in S_{d}\mid\tau\leq\sigma_{\lambda}\right\},

where
\sigma_{\lambda}
was defined in (
2.1.1
). By
[
3
, Lemma 1.5]
, the function

\displaystyle\eta:\mathcal{D}^{st}_{\lambda}\to\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\tau\mapsto t^{\lambda}.\tau

is a bijection. Define a partial order on
\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
by declaring
\eta(\tau_{1})\leq\eta(\tau_{2})
if and only if
\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}
. We refer to this as the weak Bruhat order on
\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
.

For example, with respect to the weak Bruhat order, the identity element
e_{S_{d}}\in S_{d}
is the unique minimal element of
\mathcal{D}^{st}_{\lambda}
. The corresponding minimal element of
\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
is
t_{0}=t^{\lambda}
. At the other extreme,
\sigma_{\lambda}
is the unique maximal element of
\mathcal{D}^{st}_{\lambda}
and
t^{\lambda}.\sigma_{\lambda}=(t^{\lambda^{\mathtt{T}}})^{\mathtt{T}}
is the corresponding maximal element of
\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
. By definition, the Hasse diagram for this partial order on
\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
is the labeled directed graph with an edge labeled by
i
from
t_{1}
to
t_{2}
whenever
t_{1}\leq_{i}t_{2}
.

Remark 2.4.1
.

It is well-known that for
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in S_{d}
,
\tau_{1}\leq_{i}\tau_{2}
if and only if
\tau_{2}=\tau_{1}s_{i}
and
\tau_{2}
has one more inversion than
\tau_{1}
(e.g., see
[
1
, Section 3.1]
). Via the bijection
\eta
this translates into the statement that for
t_{1},t_{2}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
,
t_{1}\leq_{i}t_{2}
if and only if
t_{2}=t_{1}.s_{i}
and
i
occurs before
i+1
when reading the entries of
t_{1}
left-to-right, top-to-bottom.

We record some basic properties of the weak Bruhat order on
\operatorname{Std}((n+r,n)^{\mathtt{T}})
which will be useful later.

Lemma 2.4.2
.

Let
\lambda=(n+r,n)
. Let
t,u,u_{1},t_{p}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda^{\mathtt{T}})
for
p=1,2,3
.

(1)

If
t\leq_{i}t_{1}
and
t\leq_{j}t_{2}
with
i\neq j
, then
|i-j|>1
.

(2)

If
t\leq_{i}t_{1}
and
t\leq_{j}t_{2}
with
i\neq j
, then there is a
t_{3}
such that
t_{1}\leq_{j}t_{3}
and
t_{1}\leq_{i}t_{3}
.

(3)

If
t_{1}\leq_{i}t_{2}\leq_{j}t_{3}
, then
i\neq j
.

(4)

If
t\leq u
,
t\leq_{i}t_{1}
, and
u\leq_{i}u_{1}
, then
t_{1}\leq u_{1}

Proof.

For the first claim, the proof of the same statement for
\lambda=(n,n)
given in
[
24
, Lemma 3.3]
applies verbatim once one accounts for the transpose map. The second claim is immediate from the first claim and the definition of the weak Bruhat order. The third claim follows directly from the definition of the weak Bruhat order. Given the previous claims the fourth claim is proven exactly as in
[
24
, Lemma 5.6]

∎

2.5.
The standard basis for Specht modules

By
[
3
, Theorem 5.6]
the set

\left\{z_{\lambda}T_{\tau}\mid\tau\in\mathcal{D}^{st}_{\lambda^{\mathtt{T}}}\right\}

is a basis for
S^{\lambda}
. Relabeling this basis using the bijection
\eta
gives a basis for
S^{\lambda}
indexed by the standard tableaux of shape
\lambda^{\mathtt{T}}
:

(2.5.1)

\left\{v_{t}:=z_{\lambda}T_{\eta^{-1}(t)}\mid t\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda^{\mathtt{T}})\right\}.

This is the so-called
standard basis
of
S^{\lambda}
. It could also be called the
polytabloid basis
for
S^{\lambda}
since at
q=1
this basis specializes to the polytabloid basis of the corresponding Specht module for the symmetric group
S_{d}
.

The weak Bruhat order can be transported to the standard basis by declaring
v_{t_{1}}\leq v_{t_{2}}
if and only if
t_{1}\leq t_{2}
in
\operatorname{Std}(\lambda^{\mathtt{T}})
. For example,

(2.5.2)

v_{0}:=v_{t_{0}}=v_{t^{\lambda^{\mathtt{T}}}}=z_{\lambda}

is the unique minimal basis element of
S^{\lambda}
and
v_{(t^{\lambda})^{\mathtt{T}}}=v_{t^{\lambda^{\mathtt{T}}}.\sigma_{\lambda^{\mathtt{T}}}}=z_{\lambda}T_{\sigma_{\lambda^{\mathtt{T}}}}
is the unique maximal basis element of
S^{\lambda}
. Note, the appearance of the tranpose of
\lambda
and the right weak Bruhat order are both due to the fact we choose to work with right
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
-modules.

Example 2.5.1
.

Let
\lambda=(3,3)
. Then
s_{\lambda^{\mathtt{T}}}=s_{2}s_{4}s_{3}=s_{4}s_{2}s_{3}
. The Hasse diagrams for
\mathcal{D}^{st}_{\lambda^{\mathtt{T}}}
,
\operatorname{Std}(\lambda^{\mathtt{T}})
, and the standard basis for
S^{(3,3)}
, respectively, are:

3.
Webs

3.1.
Webs

We next introduce a diagrammatic description of Specht modules labeled by two-row partitions using web diagrams. An
(n+r,n)
-web diagram is a certain non-crossing pairing of the elements of the set

\left\{1,2,\dots,2n+r,1^{\prime},2^{\prime},\dots,r^{\prime}\right\}.

This description is essentially known to experts (e.g., see
[
6
,
11
]
). However, we could not find one which exactly matches our preferred conventions and so opt to give a detailed treatment.

When discussing webs we will usually view them as graphs with vertices labeled by the set
\left\{1,2,\dots,2n+r,1^{\prime},2^{\prime},\dots,r^{\prime}\right\}
and where the pairing of the vertex labeled by
a
and the vertex labeled by
b
will be represented by a line connecting these vertices. We will call that line the
edge
incident to vertex
a
and vertex
b
.

Definition 3.1.1
.

Let
n,r
be nonnegative integers. An
(n+r,n)
-web
is a graph contained in the strip
\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]
which satisfies:

•

vertices on the line
\mathbb{R}\times\{0\}
labeled by
1,\dots,2n+r
in that order when read left-to-right, vertices on the line
\mathbb{R}\times\{1\}
labeled by
1^{\prime},\dots,r^{\prime}
in that order when read left-to-right;

•

each vertex is connected by an edge to one and only one other vertex;

•

vertices labeled by
1^{\prime},\dots,r^{\prime}
are only connected to vertices labeled by
1,\dots,2n+r
;

•

the graph is planar.

The non-crossing pairing associated to such a graph is the obvious one. Graphs which are related by a planar isotopy give the same pairings and are considered equal as webs.

For brevity’s sake, given a web and a
k\in\left\{1,\dotsc,2n+r,1^{\prime},\dotsc,r^{\prime}\right\}
, we may say vertex
k
to mean the vertex labeled by
k
in the given web. We will frequently leave the vertices labels implicit when it cannot cause confusion. On the right-hand-side of
Eq.

4.2.6
one can find an example of a
(10,6)
-web and in
Section

6.1
are various examples of
(3,3)
-webs. The following is an
(n+r,n)
-web which will play an important role in what follows:

(3.1.1)

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;\;.

If an edge in an
(n+r,n)
-web connects two vertices from among
\{1,\dots,2n+r\}
we call it an
arc
. If the edge connects a vertex from among
\{1,\dots,2n+r\}
to a vertex from among
\{1^{\prime},\dots,r^{\prime}\}
, then we call it a
through-string
.
For an arc, the
L(eft)-vertex
(resp.,
R(ight)-vertex
) of the arc is the leftmost (resp., rightmost) vertex adjacent to the arc. For a through-string, the
L-vertex
is the vertex with label from the set
\{1,\dotsc,2n+r\}
and the
R-vertex
is the one with label from the set
\{1^{\prime},\dotsc,r^{\prime}\}
. Given a pair
a,b\in\left\{1,2,\dots,2n+r,1^{\prime},2^{\prime},\dots,r^{\prime}\right\}
which are connected by an edge in some web we write
\langle a,b\rangle
for that edge and usually assume that
a
is the L-vertex and
b
is the R-vertex.

3.2.
Webs and standard tableaux

Given
n,r\geq 0
, let
\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,r)}
be the set of
(n+r,n)
-webs. Let
\lambda=(n+r,n)
, viewed as a partition of
2n+r
. Define

\phi:\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,n)}\to\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)

by setting
\phi(w)
to be the standard tableau of shape
\lambda
obtained by filling the boxes in the first row (resp., second row) of
\lambda
with the labels of the L-vertices (resp., R-vertices of arcs) of
w
read left-to-right. By definition
\phi(w)
is a row-standard tableau of shape
\lambda
. That
\phi(w)
is standard and that
\phi
is a bijection will be verified in
Lemma

3.2.2
.

On the other hand, define

\psi:\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)\to\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,n)}

by the following algorithm. By construction
\psi(t)
will be a pairing of the elements of the set
\left\{1,\dotsc,2n+r,1^{\prime},\dotsc,r^{\prime}\right\}
. We will show in
Lemma

3.2.2
that
\psi(t)
is an
(n+r,n)
-web and that
\psi
is a bijection.

Given a standard tableau of shape
\lambda
,
t
, let
\psi(t)
be the graph constructed recursively as follows:

Step 0:

Mark vertices on the line
\mathbb{R}\times\{0\}
labeled by
1,\dots,2n+r
in that order when read left-to-right and vertices on the line
\mathbb{R}\times\{1\}
labeled by
1^{\prime},\dots,r^{\prime}
in that order when read left-to-right;

Step 1:

Consider the entries in the second row of
t
and consider those which label a vertex of the graph under construction which are not yet adjacent to an edge. If there is no such entry, go to Step 3. Otherwise, let
b
be the entry of the leftmost such box of
t
.

Step 2:

Consider the set of vertices in the graph under construction which are to the left of the vertex
b
and which have labels which appear in the first row of
t
. Connect vertex
b
to the vertex from this collection which is closest to vertex
b
in the graph and not yet adjacent to an edge. Return to Step 1.

Step 3:

At this step there are
r
vertices labeled by
1,\dots,2n+r
which are not yet adjacent to an edge. Connect these vertices to the vertices labeled by
1^{\prime},\dots,r^{\prime}
using non-crossing edges.

Example 3.2.1
.

Say
n=2
,
r=1
and
t
is the following tableau of shape
(3,2)
:

t=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.40881pt\vbox{\kern 0.2044pt\hbox{\kern 0.40881pt\vbox to8.43109pt{\vss\hbox to8.02228pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 1$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.43109pt\vrule height=8.43109pt,width=0.40881pt\kern 8.02228pt\vrule height=8.43109pt,width=0.40881pt}\kern-0.2044pt\kern-8.43109pt\hrule width=8.8399pt,height=0.40881pt\kern 8.02228pt\hrule width=8.8399pt,height=0.40881pt}&\lower 0.40881pt\vbox{\kern 0.2044pt\hbox{\kern 0.40881pt\vbox to8.43109pt{\vss\hbox to8.02228pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 2$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.43109pt\vrule height=8.43109pt,width=0.40881pt\kern 8.02228pt\vrule height=8.43109pt,width=0.40881pt}\kern-0.2044pt\kern-8.43109pt\hrule width=8.8399pt,height=0.40881pt\kern 8.02228pt\hrule width=8.8399pt,height=0.40881pt}&\lower 0.40881pt\vbox{\kern 0.2044pt\hbox{\kern 0.40881pt\vbox to8.43109pt{\vss\hbox to8.02228pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 3$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.43109pt\vrule height=8.43109pt,width=0.40881pt\kern 8.02228pt\vrule height=8.43109pt,width=0.40881pt}\kern-0.2044pt\kern-8.43109pt\hrule width=8.8399pt,height=0.40881pt\kern 8.02228pt\hrule width=8.8399pt,height=0.40881pt}\cr\lower 0.40881pt\vbox{\kern 0.2044pt\hbox{\kern 0.40881pt\vbox to8.43109pt{\vss\hbox to8.02228pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 4$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.43109pt\vrule height=8.43109pt,width=0.40881pt\kern 8.02228pt\vrule height=8.43109pt,width=0.40881pt}\kern-0.2044pt\kern-8.43109pt\hrule width=8.8399pt,height=0.40881pt\kern 8.02228pt\hrule width=8.8399pt,height=0.40881pt}&\lower 0.40881pt\vbox{\kern 0.2044pt\hbox{\kern 0.40881pt\vbox to8.43109pt{\vss\hbox to8.02228pt{\hss$\scriptstyle 5$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.43109pt\vrule height=8.43109pt,width=0.40881pt\kern 8.02228pt\vrule height=8.43109pt,width=0.40881pt}\kern-0.2044pt\kern-8.43109pt\hrule width=8.8399pt,height=0.40881pt\kern 8.02228pt\hrule width=8.8399pt,height=0.40881pt}\crcr}}\kern 867.63998pt}}$}

Let us explain how the algorithm yields this web. In Step 0 the labeled vertices are placed as indicated. The first time Step 1 is applied, vertex
4
is the leftmost entry of the second row of
\lambda
which is not yet adjacent to an edge and so is selected. In Step 2 we connect vertex
4
to vertex
3
since from among the vertices labeled by entries from the first row of
\lambda
which are to the left of
4
and not yet adjacent to an edge (namely, vertices
1
,
2
, and
3
), this is the nearest. Having completed Step 2, we return to Step 1. In this iteration, in Step 1 we select vertex
5
since it is the leftmost entry of the second row of
\lambda
which is not yet adjacent to an edge. In Step 2 we connect vertex
5
to vertex
2
since from among the vertices labeled by entries from the first row of
\lambda
which are to the left of
5
are not yet adjacent to an edge (namely, vertices
1
and
2
), this is the nearest. Having completed Step 2, we return to Step 1. In this iteration, since all entries of the second row of
\lambda
are now adjacent to an edge we go to Step 3. There is one remaining open
L
-vertex, namely vertex
1
, and we connect it to vertex
1^{\prime}
by a through-string. The algorithm stops and the result is the indicated web.

Two remarks are in order. First, by construction the entries in the first (resp., second) row of
t
will be the labels for the L-vertices (resp., R-vertices of arcs) of
\psi(t)
. Second, say we are applying Step 2 of the algorithm for the
k
th time and
b_{k}
is the selected entry of the second row of
t
. There are
k
vertex labels in the first row of
t
which are weakly to the left of the box containing
b_{k}
. Since
t
is standard, all of these entries are smaller than
b_{k}
. That is, in the graph under construction there are at least
k
eventual L-vertices to the left of vertex
b_{k}
in the graph under construction. That is, when we apply Step 2 to the box containing
b_{k}
, we will be in the
k
th iteration of Step 2, have so far drawn
k-1
arcs, and there remains at least one open L-vertex to the left of vertex
b_{k}
which is not yet adjacent to an edge. That is, for each box in the second row of
t
, the set of L-vertices considered when Step 2 is applied to the entry of that box will be nonempty and, hence, there will be a nearest one. In short, the algorithm never fails in Step 2 and, hence, never fails.

Lemma 3.2.2
.

The images of the maps
\phi
and
\psi
lie in the given sets and the two maps are mutual inverses. In particular,

|\mathcal{W}^{(n+r+r)}|=|\operatorname{Std}(n+r,n)|.

Proof.

We first explain why the tableau
\phi(w)
is standard for any
(n+r,n)
-web
w
. First, by definition the entries of
\phi(w)
are increasing along rows. Now consider the columns of
\phi(w)
. Say
a_{k}
and
b_{k}
are the entries in the
k
th column of
\phi(w)
and are in the first and second rows, respectively. From the definition of
\phi
it follows that
b_{k}
is the
k
th R-vertex of an arc as one reads left-to-right in
w
. That is, there are at least
k
L-vertices to the left of the vertex
b_{k}
. Thus, the first
k
entries of the first row of
\phi(w)
are filled with labels which are smaller than
b_{k}
. In particular,
a_{k}
is smaller than
b_{k}
. Since this is true for every column,
\phi(w)
is standard.

We next explain why
\psi(t)
is an
(n+r,n)
-web for any standard tableau
t
of shape
\lambda=(n+r,n)
. By construction,
\psi(t)
provides a pairing of the vertices
\{1,\dots,2n+r,1^{\prime},\dots,r^{\prime}\}
and the vertices
1^{\prime},\dots,r^{\prime}
are only connected to vertices from among
\{1,\dots,2n+r\}
.

It remains to confirm that the graph is planar. By construction any two through-strings are non-crossing, so the only worry is the crossing of two arcs, or of an arc and a through-string. First, consider two crossing arcs. Let
\langle a_{k},b_{k}\rangle
and
\langle a_{\ell},b_{\ell}\rangle
be two arcs which cross, where
a_{k}
and
a_{\ell}
are the L-vertices,
b_{k}
and
b_{\ell}
are the
R
-vertices, and assume without loss of generality that
b_{k}<b_{\ell}
. Since the arcs cross it must be that
a_{k}<a_{\ell}<b_{k}
. Since
b_{k}<b_{\ell}
, we know the arc
\langle a_{k},b_{k}\rangle
was drawn before the arc
\langle a_{\ell},b_{\ell}\rangle
. At the time the arc
\langle a_{k},b_{k}\rangle
was drawn, both
a_{k}
and
a_{\ell}
were eventual L-vertices to the left of
b_{k}
which were not yet adjacent to an edge. But if this were the case, then the algorithm would have chosen to connect
b_{k}
to
a_{\ell}
instead of
a_{k}
, since it was available and closer to
b_{k}
. In short, the algorithm never creates a pair of crossing arcs. Entirely similar reasoning explains why the algorithm never creates the crossing of a through-string and an arc. Therefore,
\psi(t)
is always a
(n+r,n)
-web, as claimed.

We now claim that
\phi(\psi(t))=t
for any standard Young tableau
t
. The first row of
\phi(\psi(t))
is filled with the labels of the L-vertices of
\psi(t)
. However, by construction, the labels of the L-vertices of
\psi(t)
are precisely the entries of the first row of
t
. Thus the entries of the first row of
\phi(\psi(t))
and
t
are the same. Likewise, the second rows of
\phi(\psi(t))
and
t
coincide. But two standard tableaux of the same shape with the same entries in each row are necessarily equal.

Finally, we claim
\psi(\phi(w))=w
for any
(n+r,n)
-web
w
. By construction the sets of L-vertices of
\psi(\phi(w))
and
w
coincide, as do the sets of R-vertices. If
\psi(\phi(w))
and
w
were to differ in their arcs this would mean there is an R-vertex which is connected to different L-vertices in
\psi(\phi(w))
and
w
. Let
b
the leftmost such R-vertex. Say
a_{1}
is the L-vertex connected to
b
in
\psi(\phi(w))
and say
a_{2}
is the
L
-vertex connected to
b
in
w
. We will assume
a_{1}<a_{2}
and leave the other case to the reader. If the edge adjacent to
a_{2}
in
\psi(\phi(w))
is a through-string, then this through-string would cross the arc
\langle a_{1},b\rangle
. Therefore
a_{2}
is the L-vertex of an arc in
\psi(\phi(w))
, say
\langle a_{2},c\rangle
. Since by assumption
\langle a_{1},b\rangle
is an arc of
\psi(\phi(w))
we know
c\neq b
. If
c>b
, then the arc
\langle a_{2},c\rangle
would cross the arc
\langle a_{1},b\rangle
. If
c<b
, then
c
would be an
R
-vertex to the left of
b
which is connected to two different
L
-vertices in
\psi(\phi(w))
and
w
, contradicting our choice of
b
. Thus,
\psi(\phi(w))
and
w
have the same set of arcs. But if two webs have the same set of arcs, then it must be that their through-strings also agree and, hence,
\psi(\phi(w))=w
.
∎

3.3.
Nesting number

The following generalizes the notion of nesting number defined in
[
24
]
.

Let
w
be an
(n+r,n)
-web. Given an arc
e=\langle a,b\rangle
in
w
, set

\operatorname{nest}(e)=\left(\#\text{ arcs $\langle a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\rangle$ in $w$ with $a^{\prime}<a<b<b^{\prime}$}\right)+\left(\#\text{ through-strings $\langle a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\rangle$ in $w$ with $a^{\prime}<a$}\right).

The
nesting number
of the web
w
is given by

\operatorname{nest}(w)=\sum_{\text{$e$ is an arc of $w$}}\operatorname{nest}(e).

If
e=\langle a,b\rangle
is an arc in
w
, set

\operatorname{nest}^{\prime}(e)=\left(\#\text{ arcs $\langle a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\rangle$ in $w$ with $a<a^{\prime}<b^{\prime}<b$}\right),

and if
e=\langle a,b\rangle
is a through-string in
w
, set

\operatorname{nest}^{\prime}(e)=\left(\#\text{ arcs $\langle a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\rangle$ in $w$ with $a<a^{\prime}<b^{\prime}$}\right).

Then, obviously,

\operatorname{nest}(w)=\sum_{\text{$e$ is an edge of $w$}}\operatorname{nest}^{\prime}(e).

For example, the
(9,3)
-web given on the right-hand side of
Eq.

4.2.6
has nesting number
3
.

3.4.
The web module

Given nonnegative integers
n
and
r
, let
W^{(n+r,n)}
be the
\mathbbm{k}
-vector space with basis given by the set of
(n+r,n)
-webs,
\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,n)}
. Define an action of
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
on
W^{(n+r,n)}
as follows:

•

If
i
and
i+1
are both through-strings, then
wT_{i}=qw
.

•

If
i
and
i+1
are not both through-strings, then define the action of
T_{i}
on
w
by considering the following linear combination of graphs obtained via concatenation on the bottom of
w
:

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-47.18799pt}{-28.99117pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

After concatenation there may be closed connected components which do not involve boundary vertices. These should be deleted and for each one which is deleted the remaining diagram should be scaled by
-[2]_{q}=-(q+q^{-1})
. After all such components are deleted the result will be a linear combination of
(n+r,n)
-webs.

Checking case-by-case verifies that the defining relations of
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
are satisfied and, hence, the following result holds.

Lemma 3.4.1
.

The action given above makes
W^{(n+r,n)}
into a well-defined right
\operatorname{H}_{2n+r}(q)
-module.

3.5.
A partial order on webs

If
w
is a
(n+r,n)
-web and
i=1,\dotsc,2n+r-1
, then set

wE_{i}=wT_{i}-qw.

The following list summarizes the possible ways
E_{i}
could act on a web
w
and the resulting nesting number. Each of these is an elementary diagrammatic calculation which we leave to the reader.

(1)

If
i
and
i+1
are both L-vertices of
w
and are adjacent to through-strings, then
wE_{i}=0
;

(2)

If
i
and
i+1
are both L-vertices of
w
and exactly one is adjacent to a through-string, then it is necessarily
i
. In this case,
wE_{i}=w^{\prime}
is an
(n+r,n)
-web with
\operatorname{nest}(w^{\prime})<\operatorname{nest}(w)
;

(3)

If
i
and
i+1
are both L-vertices of
w
and both are adjacent to arcs, then
wE_{i}=w^{\prime}
is an
(n+r,n)
-web with
\operatorname{nest}(w^{\prime})<\operatorname{nest}(w)
;

(4)

If
i
is an L-vertex and
i+1
is an R-vertex, then necessarily an arc connects
i
to
i+1
and
wE_{i}=-[2]_{q}w
with the nesting number unchanged;

(5)

If
i
is an R-vertex and
i+1
is a L-vertex, then
wE_{i}=w^{\prime}
is an
(n+r,n)
-web with
\operatorname{nest}(w^{\prime})=\operatorname{nest}(w)+1
;

(6)

If
i
and
i+1
are both R-vertices, then
wE_{i}=w^{\prime}
is an
(n+r,n)
-web with
\operatorname{nest}(w^{\prime})<\operatorname{nest}(w)
;

A contemplation of the above cases shows that there is only one case when the action of
E_{i}
increases the nesting number of a web. If
w,w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,r)}
, write

w\preceq_{i}w^{\prime}

if
wE_{i}=w^{\prime}
and
\operatorname{nest}(w^{\prime})>\operatorname{nest}(w)
. Define a partial order
\preceq
on
\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,n)}
by writing
w\preceq w^{\prime}
if
w=w^{\prime}
or if there are sequences
w_{1},\dotsc,w_{t}\in\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,n)}
and
1\leq i_{1},\dotsc,i_{t}\leq 2n+r-1
such that

w\preceq_{i_{1}}w_{1}\preceq_{i_{2}}w_{2}\preceq_{i_{3}}\dotsb\preceq_{i_{t}}w_{t}=w^{\prime}.

The Hasse diagram for this partial order is the set
\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,n)}
with a directed edge labeled by
i
from
w
to
w^{\prime}
whenever
w\preceq_{i}w^{\prime}
.

Observe that the web
w_{0}
given in
Eq.

3.1.1
is the unique
(n+r,n)
-web with nesting number zero. Therefore it is the unique minimal element in
\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,n)}
with respect to the partial order
\preceq
. As another example, the following web is the unique maximal element and is the only one to have nesting number
\frac{n(n-1)}{2}+rn
:

\hss}\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill}

\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;.

Note that whenever
w\preceq_{i}w^{\prime}
, one has
\operatorname{nest}(w^{\prime})=\operatorname{nest}(w)+1
. Thus
\operatorname{nest}(w)
equals the length of any path from
w_{0}
to
w
in the Hasse diagram for
\preceq
.

Proposition 3.5.1
.

Let
\lambda=(n+r,n)
. For all
w,w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}
and all
1\leq i<2n+r
, the bijection

(-)^{\mathtt{T}}\circ\phi:\mathcal{W}^{\lambda}\to\operatorname{Std}(\lambda^{\mathtt{T}})

satisfies

\phi(w)^{\mathtt{T}}\leq_{i}\phi(w^{\prime})^{\mathtt{T}}\text{ if and only if }w\preceq_{i}w^{\prime}.

Proof.

Say
w\preceq_{i}w^{\prime}
. Since
wE_{i}=w^{\prime}
, the sets of
L
-vertices and
R
-vertices of
w
and
w^{\prime}
are identical with one exception: in
w
,
i
is an R-vertex and
i+1
is an L-vertex, while in
w^{\prime}
these are reversed. Consequently the entries of the first row of
\phi(w)
and
\phi(w^{\prime})
are identical except that the first contains
i+1
while the second contains
i
. The same statement with
i
and
i+1
reversed applies to their second rows, as well. As a consequence,
\phi(w)^{\mathtt{T}}.s_{i}=\phi(w^{\prime})^{\mathtt{T}}
. Furthermore, since both
\phi(w)
and
\phi(w^{\prime})
are standard, it must be that
i
appears in a column of
\phi(w)
which is to strictly to the left of the column containing
i+1
. That is,
i
occurs earlier than
i+1
when reading the entries of
\phi(w)^{\mathtt{T}}
from left-to-right, top-to-bottom. As discussed in
Remark

2.4.1
, this shows
\phi(w)^{\mathtt{T}}\leq_{i}\phi(w^{\prime})^{\mathtt{T}}
.

Now say
\phi(w)^{\mathtt{T}}\leq_{i}\phi(w^{\prime})^{\mathtt{T}}
. Then
\phi(w)^{\mathtt{T}}.s_{i}=\phi(w^{\prime})^{\mathtt{T}}
and
i
occurs earlier than
i+1
when reading the entries of
\phi(w)^{\mathtt{T}}
from left-to-right, top-to-bottom. Since both
\phi(w)^{\mathtt{T}}
and
\phi(w^{\prime})^{\mathtt{T}}
are standard, it cannot be that
i
and
i+1
are in the same row or column of
\phi(w)^{\mathtt{T}}
. Furthermore, if
i
were to be in the first column of
\phi(w)^{\mathtt{T}}
and
i+1
in the second column of
\phi(w)^{\mathtt{T}}
, then since
i
occurs earlier than
i+1
when reading the entries of
\phi(w)^{\mathtt{T}}
, it would be that
i
is strictly above
i+1
in
\phi(w)^{\mathtt{T}}
. But in this case there would be an entry of
\phi(w)^{\mathtt{T}}
which is below
i
and to the left of
i+1
and this entry would be larger than
i
and smaller than
i+1
. As this cannot happen, it must be that
i
is in the second column of
\phi(w)^{\mathtt{T}}
and
i+1
is in the first column of
\phi(w)^{\mathtt{T}}
. That is,
i
is in the second row of
\phi(w)
and
i+1
is in the first row of
\phi(w)
. That is,
i
is an
R
-vertex of
w
and
i+1
is a
L
-vertex of
w
. Hence,
\operatorname{nest}(w)<\operatorname{nest}(wE_{i})
and
w\preceq_{i}wE_{i}
.

It remains to verify that
wE_{i}=w^{\prime}
. On the one hand, the sets of
L
-vertices and
R
-vertices of
w
and
wE_{i}
are identical with one exception: in
w
,
i
is a R-vertex and
i+1
is an L-vertex, while in
wE_{i}
these are reversed. That is,
\phi(wE_{i})=\phi(w).s_{i}
. But since
\phi(w)^{\mathtt{T}}\leq_{i}\phi(w^{\prime})^{\mathtt{T}}
, it is also true that
\phi(w^{\prime})=\phi(w).s_{i}
. Since
\phi
is a bijection, the claim follows.
∎

In particular,
(-)^{\mathtt{T}}\circ\phi
defines an isomorphism of directed, labeled graphs between the Hasse diagrams
(\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,n)},\preceq)
and
(\operatorname{Std}(\lambda^{\mathtt{T}}),\leq)
and, hence, for
w\in\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,n)}
,

\operatorname{nest}(w)=\ell(\eta^{-1}(\phi(w)^{\mathtt{T}})).

Remark 3.5.2
.

Using the previous proposition, the statements about the weak Bruhat order on
\operatorname{Std}((n+r,n)^{\mathtt{T}})
given in
Lemma

2.4.2
directly translate to statements about the partial order on
\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,n)}
. In
Section

5
we use the web versions of these results without further comment.

Lemma 3.5.3
.

Let
w,w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,n)}
be one of the following pairs of non-crossing webs. In each of the pairs only a local region of the webs is drawn, it is assumed that
w
and
w^{\prime}
are identical other than as indicated, and that every edge adjacent to a vertex in the region is represented in the diagram. Finally, in the given diagrams,
d_{1}
,
d_{2}
, and
d_{3}
are possibly empty, fixed, non-crossing webs consisting of only arcs.

(1)

For each pair,

w\preceq w^{\prime}.

Proof.

For the pairs in (1), (2), and (3), since the argument is local and only involves arcs, the claim follows from
[

, Lemma 4.9, Theorem 4.10]
.

d_{2}
is empty. In which case
q=i+1
. Then

is an

-vertex,
q
is an

-vertex, and so
w\preceq wE_{i}
. Furthermore,

By repeatedly using (1) on the subdiagram obtained by excluding the through-string which connects vertex
p
to vertex
k^{\prime}
, one can move the arc
\langle i,i+1\rangle
so that it covers
d_{1}
and obtain
w^{\prime}
. That is, one can construct a chain of webs
wE_{i}\preceq w_{1}\preceq w_{2}\preceq\dotsb\preceq w^{\prime}
. It follows that
w\preceq w^{\prime}
in the case when
d_{2}
is empty.

To obtain the general case one only need observe that in general
d_{2}
consists of the horizontal concatenation of subdiagrams which look like:

where
d_{3}
is a possibly empty non-crossing web. By starting with
w
and repeatedly applying the result of the previous paragraph, the through-string can be “moved” past each of these subdiagrams and, hence, past
d_{2}
. Using the result one more time to move the through-string past the arc containing
d_{1}
yields
w^{\prime}
. In short, there is once again a chain of webs
w\preceq w_{1}\preceq w_{2}\preceq\dotsb\preceq w^{\prime}
and the result follows.
∎

By the first we mean that, by definition, a web with a crossing of two edges as indicated is shorthand for the linear combination of two webs obtained by resolving the crossing using the equality given in
Eq.

3.6.1
and otherwise leaving the web unchanged. Similarly, the second is a shorthand for whenever a web has an isolated connected component (a “bubble”) it is equal to the web obtained by deleting the component and scaling the resulting web by
-[2]_{q}
. Going forward it is also convenient to allow for webs with one or more edges connecting a pair of vertices from among
\{1^{\prime},\dotsc,r^{\prime}\}
by declaring such webs are equal to zero.

For example, the diagram

is a shorthand for the following linear combination of non-crossing
(2+1,2)
-webs:

From now on, an
(n+r,n)
-web is allowed to have finitely many crossings and bubbles. If we need to emphasize that an
(n+r,n)
-web is in fact an element of
\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,n)}
, then we will refer to it as a non-crossing web.

The action of
\operatorname{H}_{2n+r}(q)
on
W^{(n+r,n)}
is particularly easy to describe using this expanded set of webs. Namely, given
m\geq 1
and
1\leq i\leq n-1
, define

where there are a total of
m
edges with only the
i
th and
(i+1)
th edges crossed. Given an
(n+r,n)
-web
w
and
1\leq i\leq 2n+r-1
, write
w\beta_{i}
for the web given by concatenating
\beta_{i}
onto the bottom of
w
. Since we declare that webs with an edge connecting vertices from among
\{1^{\prime},\dotsc,r^{\prime}\}
are zero,
wT_{i}=w\beta_{i}
in
W^{(n+r,n)}
.

It is a satisfying exercise to verify the defining relations of
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
using the crossing and bubble relations. These relations can also be used to obtain the following version of the Reidemeister II move.

Lemma 3.6.1
.

In
W^{(n+r,n)}
the following local relations hold:

Remark 3.6.2
.

Note that the definition of
L
-vertex and
R
-vertex directly generalizes to
(n+r,n)
-webs with crossings and bubbles. In particular, for any
(n+r,n)
-web
w
and any
1\leq i\leq 2n+r-1
, the
L
- and
R
-vertices of
w\beta_{i}
are the same as for
w
except that the vertex type of
i
and
i+1
are interchanged (e.g., if in
w

i
is an
R
-vertex and
i+1
is an
L
-vertex, then in
w\beta_{i}

i
is an
L
-vertex and
i+1
is an
R
-vertex).

We end this section by giving names to several local diagrams which will be relevant in the sequel. By “local diagram” we mean an area of a possibly larger web such that within this region it looks as depicted. The local diagrams shown below will be called
bigons
,
triangles
, and
squares
, respectively:

The labels N, S, E, W are for later reference and can be ignored for now.

4.
Jimbo–Schur–Weyl Duality and Tubbenhauer–Vaz–Wedrich Diagrams

4.1.
Jimbo–Schur–Weyl duality

Since
W^{(n+r,r)}
is a right
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
-module with the same dimension as
S^{(n+r,r)}
, it will come as little surprise that they are isomorphic. Proving this claim is the goal of this section. First we record some well-known results (e.g., see
[
2
,
12
]
).

Let
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{2})
be the quantized enveloping algebra defined over
\mathbbm{k}
which is associated to
\mathfrak{gl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
. Let
V=\mathbbm{k}^{2}
be the natural module for
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{2})
with
\{v_{0},v_{1}\}
the standard basis. Define the linear map
\beta:V\otimes V\to V\otimes V
by

\beta(v_{a}\otimes v_{b})=\begin{cases}qv_{a}\otimes v_{b},&\text{if $a=b$};\\
v_{b}\otimes v_{a},&\text{if $a<b$};\\
v_{b}\otimes v_{a}+(q-q^{-1})v_{a}\otimes v_{b},&\text{if $a>b$}.\end{cases}

This is the
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{2})
-linear endomorphism defined by the
R
-matrix (e.g., see
[
12
, VIII.1]
) and satisfies the equation
(\beta-q)(\beta+q^{-1})=0
. Given
\lambda\in\Lambda_{+}(2)
, let
L_{q}(\lambda)
be the simple
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{2})
-module indexed by
\lambda
. Let
S_{q}^{a}(V)
(resp.,
\Lambda_{q}^{a}(V)
) be the
a
th symmetric power (resp., exterior power) of
V
for any
a\geq 0
. For example,
S_{q}^{2}(V)\cong L_{q}(2,0)
and can be realized as the
q
-eigenspace of the endomorphism
\beta
.

Since
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{2})
is a Hopf algebra,
V^{\otimes d}
is a left
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{2})
-module. Jimbo–Schur–Weyl duality states that for every
d\geq 0
there is a surjective algebra homomorphism

\Phi_{d}:\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)\to\operatorname{End}_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{2})}\left(V^{\otimes d}\right)

defined by
\Phi_{d}(T_{i})=1_{V}^{\otimes(i-1)}\otimes\beta\otimes 1_{V}^{\otimes(d-i-1)}
. Consequently, for any left
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{2})
-module,
M
,
\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{2})}(V^{\otimes d},M)
is a right
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
-module with the action of
x\in\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
given by pre-composition by
\Phi_{d}(x)
. In particular, as explained in
[
4
, Remark 2.1(ii), Proposition 4.5.8]
, for every
\lambda\in\Lambda_{+}(2,d)
there is an isomorphism of right
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
-modules:

(4.1.1)

\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{2})}\left(V^{\otimes d},L_{q}(\lambda)\right)\cong S^{\lambda}.

4.2.
Tubbenhauer–Vaz–Wedrich diagrams

Let
2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}
be the green-red diagrammatic category for
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{2})
given by
[
25
, Definition 2.5]
. Briefly, this is a
\mathbbm{k}
-linear monoidal category which is defined by generators and relations. The objects are words from the set
\left\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}a},{\color[rgb]{0.1328,0.5469,0.1328}b},1\mid a,b\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\right\}
. The morphisms are certain diagrams generated by the following
green
and
red
“merge” morphisms
ab\to a+b
and “split” morphisms
a+b\to ab
(for all
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
):

\hbox to22.01pt{\vbox to59.37pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 38.9243pt\lower-29.47726pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{
{

{

{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{
{}{}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{
{}{}{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0.1328,0.5469,0.1328}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -25.59 -25.59 C -25.59 -14.39 -27.19 0 -38.39 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {
{}{}{}}{}{{}}{}
{
{}{}{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{
{}{}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{
{}{}{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0.1328,0.5469,0.1328}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -51.18 -25.59 C -51.18 -14.39 -49.58 0 -38.39 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {

Observe that we use dashed lines (
) for green diagrams and densely dashdotted lines (
) for red diagrams to help distinguish them.

Also note that diagrams are read bottom-to-top. The identity of an object is parallel vertical strands sequentially labeled by the letters which make up the object word. We declare
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}=1={\color[rgb]{0.1328,0.5469,0.1328}1}
. While edges in a diagram labeled by a
1
are usually drawn as black, they can be considered either red or green as needed in order to make a valid diagram. As above, edges in a diagram are labeled by the generating object which is the (co)domain of the relevant generating morphism. Unmarked edges are understood to have label
1
. Composition is given by vertical concatenation and the monoidal product is given by horizontal concatenation. Therefore, a general morphism will be a
\mathbbm{k}
-linear combination of diagrams made by finitely many such concatenations of the generating diagrams. The morphisms are subject to various local relations, including that any diagram which contains a green edge labeled by
{\color[rgb]{0.1328,0.5469,0.1328}k}\geq 3
is equal to zero.

For example, for each
i=1,\dotsc,d-1
, there is a morphism
\beta_{i}:1^{\otimes d}\to 1^{\otimes d}
in
2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}
given by the following linear combination of diagrams:

\beta_{i}=\beta_{i}^{d}=q\;\hbox to95.87pt{\vbox to48.35pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 94.17201pt\lower-29.35226pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{

where the first diagram has
d
black strands, and the second diagram has
d-2
black strands and the green merge and split are in the
i
and
i+1
positions.

Recall the generators and relations defining the Hecke algebra
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
as given in
Definition

2.2.1
. By
[
25
, Lemma 2.25]
, there is an algebra homomorphism

(4.2.1)

\displaystyle\zeta_{d}:\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{End}_{2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}}\left(1^{d}\right),

(4.2.2)

\displaystyle T_{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto\beta_{i}.

In particular, there is a right action of
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
on the
\mathbbm{k}
-vector space
\operatorname{Hom}_{2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}}\left(1^{d},{\color[rgb]{0.1328,0.5469,0.1328}2}^{n}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}r}\right)
for any
r,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
where
T_{i}
acts by pre-composition by
\zeta_{d}(T_{i})
.

In
[
25
, Definition 3.17]
the authors define a functor of monoidal categories,

\varGamma:2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}\to U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{2})\text{-modules}.

On objects
1\mapsto V
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}a}\mapsto S^{a}_{q}(V)
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.1328,0.5469,0.1328}b}\mapsto\Lambda_{q}^{b}(V)
. Of particular relevance to this paper,

1^{d}\mapsto V^{\otimes d}

and

{\color[rgb]{0.1328,0.5469,0.1328}2}^{n}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}r}\mapsto\Lambda_{q}^{2}(V)^{\otimes n}\otimes S^{r}_{q}(V)\cong L_{q}(n+r,n).

The isomorphism is well-known and holds, for instance, from the fact both modules are semisimple polynomial representations of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{2})
along with character considerations.

By
[
25
, Theorem 3.20]
, the functor
\varGamma
is fully faithful and so for all
d,n,r\geq 0
defines a vector space isomorphism,

(4.2.3)

\varGamma:\operatorname{Hom}_{2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}}\left(1^{d},{\color[rgb]{0.1328,0.5469,0.1328}2}^{n}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}r}\right)\xrightarrow{\cong}\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{2})}\left(V^{\otimes d},L_{q}(n+r,n)\right).

Furthermore, as explained in the proof of
[
25
, Lemma 2.25]
, the map given by
\varGamma
is an isomorphism of right
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
-modules (where the action of
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
on the latter space is given by Jimbo–Schur–Weyl duality as in the previous section). In summary, if
d=2n+r
, then this discussion along with
Eq.

4.1.1
implies

(4.2.4)

\operatorname{Hom}_{2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}}\left(1^{d},{\color[rgb]{0.1328,0.5469,0.1328}2}^{n}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}r}\right)\cong S^{(n+r,n)}

as right
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
-modules.

Proposition 4.2.1
.

For all nonnegative integers
n
and
r
, there is an isomorphism of right
\operatorname{H}_{2n+r}(q)
-modules

\varphi:S^{(n+r,n)}\xrightarrow{\cong}W^{(n+r,n)}.

Furthermore, by rescaling if necessary,
\varphi(v_{0})=w_{0}
.

Here
v_{0}=v_{t_{0}}=z_{\lambda}
is as in
Eq.

2.5.2
and
w_{0}
is the
(n+r,n)
-web given in
Eq.

3.1.1
.
We refer the reader to
Section

6.2
and
Section

6.3
for examples of the matrix for
\varphi
when
\lambda=(3,3)
and
\lambda=(4,2)
, respectively.

Proof.

Put
\lambda=(n+r,n)
and
d=2n+r
. First we prove the existence of the isomorphism. Combining
Eqs.

4.2.4
and
3.2.2
along with fact that
S^{\lambda}
is a simple
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
-module, it suffices to define a nonzero
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
-module homomorphism

\eta:\operatorname{Hom}_{2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}}\left(1^{d},{\color[rgb]{0.1328,0.5469,0.1328}2}^{n}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}r}\right)\to W^{(n+r,n)}.

Consider the subspace of
\operatorname{Hom}_{2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}}\left(1^{d},{\color[rgb]{0.1328,0.5469,0.1328}2}^{n}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}r}\right)
spanned by all diagrams of the form:

(4.2.5)

D\;\;=\;\;\hbox to102.99pt{\vbox to88.58pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 44.38055pt\lower-22.95067pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{
{

\lxSVG@closescope {

\lxSVG@closescope {

\lxSVG@closescope {

where
X
is any diagram in
\operatorname{End}_{2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}}(1^{\otimes d})
obtained by vertically and horizontally concatenating green merges, splits, and identities (recall that an edge labeled by
1
is considered to be green), and where the red diagram is
r
strands labeled by
1
that are successively merged together from left-to-right by concatenating red merges. Note that since green edges labeled by
{\color[rgb]{0.1328,0.5469,0.1328}k}\geq 3
do not appear in
2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}
, this subspace is spanned by diagrams of this form where the
X
consists of only green edges labeled by
1
’s and
{\color[rgb]{0.1328,0.5469,0.1328}2}
’s.

Since this is a nonzero subspace of
\operatorname{Hom}_{2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}}\left(1^{d},{\color[rgb]{0.1328,0.5469,0.1328}2}^{n}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}r}\right)
and is obviously stable under the right action of
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
, it must equal
\operatorname{Hom}_{2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}}\left(1^{d},{\color[rgb]{0.1328,0.5469,0.1328}2}^{n}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}r}\right)
by simplicity. Thus it suffices to define
\eta
on such diagrams. On a diagram
D
as described above,
\eta(D)
is defined by performing the following sequence of operations on
D
:

•

Delete the red merges.

•

Make the following local substitutions for the green merge and split diagrams in
X
:

•

Turn all edges black and delete all edge labels.

•

For each connected component which is not connected to vertex along the top or bottom edge, delete the component and scale the diagram by
-[2]_{q}
.

The resulting diagram will be a scalar multiple of an
(n+r,n)
-web. For example,
\eta
acts on the following diagram as indicated:

\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;.

The map
\eta
is obtained by extending this rule linearly.

We need to check that
\eta
is well-defined. First we verify that the defining relations for
2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}
given in
[
25
, Definition 2.3]
involving only green edges are preserved by
\eta
. Since diagrams with a green edge labeled by
{\color[rgb]{0.1328,0.5469,0.1328}k}\geq 3
are zero, the “digon removal relation”
[
25
, (2-7)]
in
2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}
reduces to the following equality:

In every case an easy check verifies that the rules for
\eta
do the same thing to both sides of the equations. In checking, note that the green (co)associativity relations given in
[
25
, (2-6)]
are zero and there is nothing to check.

As we next explain, the fact it preserves the green relations implies
\eta
is well-defined.

Here
E
has
n
green splits and the red diagram is an
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}r}
split apart into
r
strands labeled by
1
by repeatedly concatenating red splits. Likewise,
C
has
n
green merges and and the red diagram is
r
strands labeled by
1
that are merged together into a single
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}r}
by concatenating red merges.

If
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
are two linear combinations of diagrams, all of the form
Eq.

4.2.5
, which are equal in
2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}
, then we can concatenate an
E
on top of each to obtain
ED_{1}
and
ED_{2}
. These are elements of
\operatorname{End}_{2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}}(1^{d})
and will still be equal in
2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}
.

Applying
[
25
, Lemma 2.12, Corollary 2.13]
to the red part of
ED_{1}
and
ED_{2}
shows they can be rewritten as a linear combination of diagrams in
2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}
consisting of only green edges. That is,
ED_{1}
and
ED_{2}
are morphisms in the subcategory
2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{g}}
(the subcategory of green objects and the morphisms in
2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}
which can be expressed as a linear combination of diagrams with only green edges). However, by
[
25
, Corollary 2.16]
the category
2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{g}}
is equivalent to the “green-ification” of the category
2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{CKM}}
introduced by Cautis–Kamnitzer–Morrison. Thus, if two diagrams are equal in
2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{g}}
, they must be related by the application of a finite sequence of defining relations of
2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{CKM}}
which have been green-ified. However, a check verifies that the greenification of the defining relations of
2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{CKM}}
are nothing but relations in
2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}
which follow from the all-green defining relations.

The discussion in the previous paragraph implies there is a sequence of linear combinations of diagrams in
\operatorname{End}_{2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{g}}}(1^{\otimes d})
,

u_{1},u_{2},\dotsc,u_{p},

where each
u_{i}
consists of only green edges, each
u_{i}
is obtained from
u_{i-1}
by applying one of the green relations of
2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}
, and where
u_{1}=ED_{1}
and
u_{2}=ED_{2}
in
2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}
. Concatenating each term of this sequence on top with
C
yields the sequence,

Cu_{1},Cu_{2},\dotsc,Cu_{p},

where each
Cu_{i}
is obtained from
Cu_{i-1}
by applying one of the green relations of
2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}
to
u_{i-1}
while keeping
C
fixed.

However, note that each
Cu_{i}
is a linear combination of diagrams of the form given in
Eq.

4.2.5
and so it makes sense to apply the rule defining
\eta
to each of them. Since
\eta
satisfies the green relations of
2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}
it follows that

\eta(Cu_{1})=\eta(Cu_{2})=\dotsc=\eta(Cu_{p}).

Set
\kappa=[r]_{q}![2]_{q}^{n}
. We claim that
\eta(D_{1})=\frac{1}{\kappa}\eta(Cu_{1})
and
\eta(D_{2})=\frac{1}{\kappa}\eta(Cu_{p})
. Since the rule for
\eta
is local and
u_{1}
(resp.,
u_{p}
) is obtained by applying
[
25
, Lemma 2.12, Corollary 2.13]
only to the red part of
ED_{1}
(resp.,
ED_{2}
), it suffices to consider the case when
D_{1}=D_{2}=D
is a diagram of the form given in
Eq.

4.2.5
where
X
consists of
d
black vertical strands labeled by
1
. That is, the case when
D=C
. By
[
25
, Lemma 2.12]
,
ED=\kappa\mathcal{CL}
, where the
\mathcal{CL}
is the green-red diagram given by the left-to-right horizontal concatenation of
n
green clasps
\mathcal{CL}_{2}^{{\color[rgb]{0.1328,0.5469,0.1328}g}}
and one red clasp
\mathcal{CL}_{r}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}r}}
. By
[
25
, Corollary 2.13, Definition 2.11]
the red clasp can be rewritten into smaller red clasps and green diagrams, and by repeatedly using this recursion one ultimately obtains
u_{1}
for
ED
. Using this recursion and our convention that any web with an edge connecting two vertices from among
\{1^{\prime},\dotsc,r^{\prime}\}
is equal to zero, a straightforward induction on the size of the red clasp shows that
\eta(Cu_{1})=\kappa w_{0}
. Since
\eta(D)=w_{0}
, it follows that
\eta(D)=\frac{1}{\kappa}\eta(Cu_{1})
, as desired.

Combining the previous calculations yields

\eta(D_{1})=\frac{1}{\kappa}\eta\left(Cu_{1}\right)=\frac{1}{\kappa}\eta\left(Cu_{p}\right)=\eta(D_{2}).

Therefore
\eta
is well-defined.

To show that
\eta
is a
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
-module homomorphism requires that we analyze the two ways the generator
T_{i}
may act on the web module as described in
Section

3.4
. The easier case is when at least one of the vertices
i
and
i+1
are not adjacent to through-strings in
\eta(D)
. In this case equivariance for the generator
T_{i}
is immediate from the observation that when the rules for
\eta
are applied to the green-red diagram
\beta_{i}
the result is the linear combination of webs which gives the action of
T_{i}
on
\eta(D)
.

The second and less obvious case is when both
i
and
i+1
are through-strings in
\eta(D)
. In this case,

\eta(D.T_{i})=q\eta(D)+\eta(D\tilde{E}_{i}),

where
\tilde{E}_{i}
is the second diagram in the definition of
\beta_{i}
given in
Section

4.2
, and where
D\tilde{E}_{i}
is the green-red diagram obtained by concatenating
\tilde{E}_{i}
onto the bottom of
D
. It will suffice to show
D\tilde{E}_{i}=0
in
2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}
.

First, by the rule for
\eta
, the tops of through-strings in
\eta(D)
correspond to vertices where the red part of
D
meets
X
. Since the two through-strings in question have adjacent vertices at the bottom, they also have adjacent vertices at the top. In
D
call these top vertices
j
and
j+1
. By the associativity relation
[
25
, Definition 2.3]
the red merge diagrams in
D
can be concatenated in any order. Therefore we may assume that the lowest red merge in
D
is located at vertices
j
and
j+1
. For example, in
Eq.

4.2.6
if
i=3
, then
j=7
and the diagram as drawn already has lowest red merge at the location of vertices
j
and
j+1
; if
i=9
, then
j=9
and we would use the associativity relation for red merges to replace the red part of
D
with, for example, the same red diagram reflected across a vertical axis.

Second, since we know that in
\eta(D)
we have parallel through-strings from
i
to
j
and from
i+1
to
j+1
, it must be that in the
X
portion of
D
the corresponding green edges labeled by
1
must travel in parallel from
i
to
j
and from
i+1
to
j+1
. As seen in
Eq.

4.2.6
, the region enclosed in
X
by these two sequences of green edges may be bisected by some number of green edges labeled by
{\color[rgb]{0.1328,0.5469,0.1328}2}
. By repeatedly using the “square switch” relation given in
Eq.

4.2.7
these edges labeled by
{\color[rgb]{0.1328,0.5469,0.1328}2}
may be eliminated. The end result is that
D\tilde{E}_{i}
is equal in
2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}
to a green-red diagram which contains a subdiagram of the form:

However, by
[
25
, Lemma 2.9]
this subdiagram and, hence,
D\tilde{E}_{i}
, is equal to zero in
2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}
. Thus
\eta
is equivariant for
T_{i}
in this case as well.

Therefore
\eta
is a nonzero
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
-module homomorphism from
\operatorname{Hom}_{2\text{ -}\operatorname{Web_{gr}}}(1^{\otimes d},{\color[rgb]{0.1328,0.5469,0.1328}2}^{\otimes n}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}r})
to
W^{(n+r,n)}
. As mentioned above, this gives the desired
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
-module isomorphism

\varphi:S^{(n+r,n)}\xrightarrow{\cong}W^{(n+r,n)}.

Finally, observe that for
k=1,\dotsc,n

w_{0}T_{2k-1}=-q^{-1}w_{0}.

Therefore,

w_{0}y_{\lambda^{\mathtt{T}}}=(1+q^{-2})^{n}w_{0}.

Lemma

2.3.1
implies that, after rescaling,
\varphi(v_{0})=\varphi(v_{t_{0}})=\varphi(v_{t^{\lambda^{\mathtt{T}}}})=\varphi(z_{\lambda})=w_{0}
, as claimed.
∎

5.
Main Results

5.1.
Unitriangularity

For the remainder of the paper let

\varphi:S^{\lambda}\to W^{(n+r,n)}

be the isomorphism given by
4.2.1
. Here and in the next result, recall that we write
W^{(n+r,n)}
for the
\mathbbm{k}
-vector space with basis the set of
(n,r)
-webs,
\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,n)}
(see
Section

3.4
).

Theorem 5.1.1
.

Let
\lambda=(n+r,n)
be a partition of
2n+r
with two parts. For every
t\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda^{\mathtt{T}})
,

\varphi(v_{t})=\psi(t^{\mathtt{T}})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,n)}\\
\operatorname{nest}(w)<\operatorname{nest}(\psi(t^{\mathtt{T}}))\end{subarray}}c_{w,t}w,

where
c_{w,t}\in\mathbb{Z}[q,q^{-1}]
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the set
\operatorname{Std}(\lambda^{\mathtt{T}})
with respect to the weak Bruhat order. The base case is the unique minimal element
t^{\lambda^{\mathtt{T}}}
. This corresponds to the basis element
v_{0}=v_{t_{0}}=v_{t^{\lambda^{\mathtt{T}}}}=z_{\lambda}
. By
4.2.1
,
\varphi(v_{0})=w_{0}=\psi(t^{\mathtt{T}}_{0})
. As
w_{0}
is the unique element of
\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,n)}
with nesting number zero, the equation holds in this case.

Now let
t\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda^{\mathtt{T}})
be given and choose
t_{1}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda^{\mathtt{T}})
such that
t_{1}\leq_{i}t
for some
i=1,\dotsc,2n+r-1
. In particular,
t=t_{1}.s_{i}
and
v_{t}=v_{t_{1}}T_{i}
. Then,

\displaystyle\varphi(v_{t})

\displaystyle=\varphi(v_{t_{1}}T_{i})

\displaystyle=\varphi(v_{t_{1}})T_{i}

\displaystyle=\left(\psi(t_{1})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,n)}\\
\operatorname{nest}(w)<\operatorname{nest}(\psi(t_{1}^{\mathtt{T}}))\end{subarray}}c_{w,t_{1}}w\right)T_{i}

\displaystyle=\left(q\psi(t_{1})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,n)}\\
\operatorname{nest}(w)<\operatorname{nest}(\psi(t_{1}^{\mathtt{T}}))\end{subarray}}qc_{w,t_{1}}w\right)+\left(\psi(t_{1})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,n)}\\
\operatorname{nest}(w)<\operatorname{nest}(\psi(t_{1}^{\mathtt{T}}))\end{subarray}}c_{w,t_{1}}w\right)E_{i},

where the third equality is by the inductive assumption, and the fourth is by the definition of the action of
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)
on
W^{\lambda}
.

In the first term of the last expression, since
t_{1}<_{i}t
,
3.5.1
implies
\psi(t^{\mathtt{T}}_{1})\prec_{i}\psi(t^{\mathtt{T}})
and, as a consequence,
\operatorname{nest}(\psi(t_{1}^{\mathtt{T}}))<\operatorname{nest}(\psi(t^{\mathtt{T}}))
. Thus, every web which appears with a nonzero coefficient in this term has nesting number strictly smaller than the nesting number of
\psi(t^{\mathtt{T}})
.

In the second term of the last expression, it follows from
3.5.1
that
\psi(t_{1}^{\mathtt{T}})E_{i}=\psi(t^{\mathtt{T}})
and
\operatorname{nest}(\psi(t^{\mathtt{T}}))=\operatorname{nest}(\psi(t_{1}^{\mathtt{T}}))+1
. For the rest of the webs which appear in this term, a check of the list of possibilities given in
Section

3.5
shows that the action
E_{i}
on a web raises the nesting number by at most one. Thus the webs which appear with a nonzero coefficient in the sum after acting by
E_{i}
must have nesting number strictly smaller than the nesting number of
\psi(t^{\mathtt{T}})
.

Finally, since the action of each
T_{i}
on a web is a
\mathbb{Z}[q,q^{-1}]
-linear combination of webs, the assertion about the coefficients is immediate.
∎

Remark 5.1.2
.

Let
R=\mathbb{Z}[q,q^{-1}]
. Let
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)_{R}
be the associative, unital
R
-algebra defined by the same generators and relations as in
Definition

2.2.1
. Given
\lambda=(n+r,n)
, one can define the Specht
\operatorname{H}_{2n+r}(q)_{R}
-module
S^{\lambda}_{R}
just as in
Section

2.3
and by
[
3
, Theorem 5.6]
the standard basis is an
R
-basis for
S^{\lambda}_{R}
. Similarly, let
W^{\lambda}_{R}
be the free
R
-module spanned by the non-crossing webs,
\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,n)}
. As the action of the generators of
\operatorname{H}_{2n+r}(q)
on webs only involves coefficients from
R
, it follows that
W^{\lambda}_{R}
is an
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)_{R}
-module.

Viewing
S^{\lambda}_{R}
and
W^{\lambda}_{R}
as
R
-submodules of
S^{\lambda}
and
W^{\lambda}
, respectively, the previous result shows that the map
\varphi
restricts to define an
\operatorname{H}_{d}(q)_{R}
-module isomorphism

\varphi:S^{\lambda}_{R}\to W^{\lambda}_{R}.

Therefore the results of this paper hold over
R
and, via base change, over any integral domain
S
and any choice of invertible
q\in S
.

As in
[
24
]
, the previous result can be sharpened.

Theorem 5.1.3
.

Let
\lambda=(n+r,n)
be a partition of
2n+r
with two parts. For every
t\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda^{\mathtt{T}})
,

\varphi(v_{t})=\psi(t^{\mathtt{T}})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,n)}\\
w\prec\psi(t^{\mathtt{T}})\end{subarray}}c_{w,t}w,

where
c_{w,t}\in\mathbb{Z}[q,q^{-1}]
.

Proof.

Given the previous theorem, it suffices to show
c_{w,t}\neq 0
implies
w\preceq\psi(t^{\mathtt{T}})
. The proof of this is by induction on the set
\operatorname{Std}(\lambda^{\mathtt{T}})
with respect to the weak Bruhat order. The base case of
t=t_{0}=t^{\lambda^{\mathtt{T}}}
is trivial. For the inductive step, let
t\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda^{\mathtt{T}})
be given and choose
t_{1}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda^{\mathtt{T}})
such that
t_{1}\leq_{i}t
for some
i=1,\dotsc,2n+r-1
. By the inductive assumption,
c_{\tilde{w},t_{1}}\neq 0
implies
\tilde{w}\preceq\psi(t_{1}^{\mathtt{T}})
for any
\tilde{w}\in\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,n)}
.

Consider,

\displaystyle\varphi(v_{t})=\varphi(v_{t_{1}})T_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{w}\in\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,n)}\\
\tilde{w}\preceq\psi(t_{1}^{\mathtt{T}})\end{subarray}}c_{\tilde{w},t_{1}}\tilde{w}T_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{w}\in\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,n)}\\
\tilde{w}\preceq\psi(t_{1}^{\mathtt{T}})\end{subarray}}qc_{\tilde{w},t_{1}}\tilde{w}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{w}\in\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,n)}\\
\tilde{w}\preceq\psi(t_{1}^{\mathtt{T}})\end{subarray}}c_{\tilde{w},t_{1}}\tilde{w}E_{i}.

In order for
w\in\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,n)}
to satisfy
c_{w,t}\neq 0
, either
w=\tilde{w}
for some
\tilde{w}
in the first sum or
w=\tilde{w}E_{i}
for some
\tilde{w}
in the second sum (or both). In the first case,
w=\tilde{w}\preceq\psi(t_{1}^{\mathtt{T}})
. But since
t_{1}\leq_{i}t
, by
3.5.1

\psi(t_{1}^{\mathtt{T}})\preceq_{i}\psi(t^{\mathtt{T}})
and, hence,
w\preceq\psi(t^{\mathtt{T}})
, as desired.

In the second case,
w=\tilde{w}E_{i}
. We consider the cases listed in
Section

3.5
and how they apply to
\tilde{w}
. Obviously, Case (1) is not relevant. If we happen to be in Case (4), then
\langle i,i+1\rangle
is an arc in
\tilde{w}
,
w=-[2]_{q}\tilde{w}
, and the reasoning of the previous paragraph still applies. If we are in Case (5), then
\tilde{w}\preceq_{i}w
. But we also have
\tilde{w}\preceq\psi(t_{1}^{\mathtt{T}})
and
\psi(t_{1}^{\mathtt{T}})\preceq_{i}\psi(t^{\mathtt{T}})
. By the fourth claim in
Lemma

2.4.2
, this implies
w\preceq\psi(t^{\mathtt{T}})
, as desired.

The remaining scenario is if
w=\tilde{w}E_{i}
,
\operatorname{nest}(w)<\operatorname{nest}(\tilde{w})
, and one of Cases (2), (3), or (6) listed in
Section

3.5
applies to
\tilde{w}
. In each of these cases the desired result follows from
Lemma

3.5.3
. Namely, if we are in Case (3), then
\langle i,y\rangle
and
\langle i+1,x\rangle
are arcs and
\tilde{w}=w^{\prime}
in the third pair of
Lemma

3.5.3
(where
p
is
i
,
i
is
i+1
,
j
is
x
, and
q
is
y
), But then
w=\tilde{w}E_{i}
is the
w
of that pair and therefore
w\preceq\tilde{w}
. But since
\tilde{w}\preceq\psi(t_{1}^{\mathtt{T}})\preceq\psi(t^{\mathtt{T}})
, the desired inequality holds. If we are in Case (6), then
\langle x,i+1\rangle
and
\langle y,i\rangle
are arcs in
\tilde{w}
, again
\tilde{w}=w^{\prime}
in the third pair of
Lemma

3.5.3
(where
p
is
x
,
i
is
y
,
j
is
i
, and
q
is
i+1
),
w=\tilde{w}E_{i}
is the
w
of that pair and therefore
w\preceq\tilde{w}
. But since
\tilde{w}\preceq\psi(t_{1}^{\mathtt{T}})\preceq\psi(t^{\mathtt{T}})
, the desired inequality holds.

The last case to consider is Case (2). Then
\langle i,k^{\prime}\rangle
is a through-string,
\langle i+1,x\rangle
is an arc, and locally
\tilde{w}
looks like the last diagram given below:

The first diagram is
w=\tilde{w}E_{i}
. The first inequality holds by using the second pair of webs in
Lemma

3.5.3
, possibly multiple times, and the second inequality holds by using the fourth pair of webs in
Lemma

3.5.3
(where
p
is
i
,
p+1
is
i+1
,
i+1
is
x
, and
k^{\prime}
is
k^{\prime}
). Once again,
w\preceq\tilde{w}\preceq\psi(t_{1}^{\mathtt{T}})\preceq\psi(t^{\mathtt{T}})
, as desired.

In short, in all cases, if
c_{w,t}\neq 0
, then
w\preceq\psi(t^{\mathtt{T}})
and the assertion is proven.
∎

5.2.
Positivity

The goal of this section is to prove the following result.

Theorem 5.2.1
.

Let
\lambda=(n+r,n)
be a partition of
2n+r
. For every
t\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda^{\mathtt{T}})
,

\varphi(v_{t})=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,n)}}c_{w,t}w

with
c_{w,t}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}[q]
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}^{(n+r,n)}
.

By
Eq.

2.5.1
,
v_{t}=v_{0}T_{i_{1}}\dotsb T_{i_{r}}
where
s_{i_{1}}\dotsb s_{i_{r}}
is a reduced word in
S_{d}
and

(5.2.1)

t^{\lambda^{\mathtt{T}}}=t_{0}\leq_{i_{1}}t_{1}\leq_{i_{2}}\dotsb\leq_{i_{r}}t_{r}=t

in the weak Bruhat order. Then,

(5.2.2)

\varphi(v_{t})=\varphi(v_{0}T_{i_{1}}\dotsb T_{i_{r}})=\varphi(v_{0})T_{i_{1}}\dotsb T_{i_{r}}=w_{0}\beta_{i_{1}}\dotsb\beta_{i_{r}}.

The proof strategy is straightforward enough. The right hand side of
Eq.

5.2.2
is an
(n+r,n)
-web with crossings. Since relation
Eq.

3.6.1
only involves coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}[q]
, if the web could be rewritten into the basis of non-crossing webs using only this relation, then the same would be true of the coefficients in the resulting linear combination. However, if one simply applies
Eq.

3.6.1
to each crossing, then it is quite possible that bubbles will appear. This will generate undesirable coefficients when they are popped using relation
Eq.

3.6.2
. The example in
Section

6.1
shows how this can happen. The key observation, in that example and in general, is that the Reidemiester II moves from
Lemma

3.6.1
also have coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}[q]
and can be used along with relation
Eq.

3.6.1
to eliminate all crossings in a way which entirely avoids the dreaded bubble popping relation.

To show how this can be done we first need some terminology. Given an
(n+r,n)
-web with crossings consider the complement of its projection onto the plane. We call a connected component of the complement
bounded
if the closure of the connected component is compact. Otherwise we call it
unbounded
. Given a web, we refer to these components as bounded and unbounded regions, respectively. For example, the interior of a bubble is a bounded region.

Example 5.2.2
.

The
(3,3)
-web given in
Section

6.1
has the following projection onto the plane:

\hbox to129.04pt{\vbox to60.22pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 71.6319pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{
{
{}{}{}}{}{{}}{}
{
{}{}{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{
{}{}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{
{}{}{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 39.37 59.06 C 39.37 82.08 78.74 82.08 78.74 59.06}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {
{}{}{}}{}{{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 78.74 59.06 L 78.74 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;\;.

The region marked with a
B
is bounded whereas the other regions of the complement are path-connected to the unbounded part of the plane and so are are unbounded.

Observe that if one applies
Eq.

3.6.1
to eliminate a crossing, then in each of the two resulting webs a pair of regions have been merged together (namely, the first term merges the regions above and below the crossing while the second term merges the regions on each side of the crossing). In particular, the application of this relation does not create entirely new bounded regions. The danger lies in the existing bounded regions of the initial web as one of them may become a bubble as crossings are eliminated. Thus the proof will focus on what happens to bounded regions as relation
Eq.

3.6.1
and Reidemiester II moves are applied.

Now consider
Eq.

5.2.1
. As discussed in
Remark

2.4.1
,
t_{j-1}\leq_{i_{j}}t_{j}
if and only if
t_{j}=t_{j-1}.s_{i_{j}}
and
i_{j}
appears before
i_{j}+1
when reading the entries of
t_{j-1}
left-to-right, top-to-bottom. Since
t_{j-1},t_{j}\in\operatorname{Std}((n+r,n)^{\mathtt{T}})
, it follows that
i_{j}
is in the second column of
(n+r,n)^{\mathtt{T}}
while
i_{j}+1
is in the first column (much as in the proof of
3.5.1
). That is,
i_{j}
is in the second row of
t_{j}^{\mathtt{T}}
while
i_{j}+1
is in the first row. That is,
i_{j}
is an
R
-vertex of
\psi(t_{j}^{\mathtt{T}})

while
i_{j}+1
is an
L
-vertex. That is, for each
j=1,\dotsc,r
, when
\beta_{i_{j}}
is concatenated on the bottom of
w_{0}\beta_{i_{1}}\dotsb\beta_{i_{j-1}}
in the construction of
\varphi(v_{t})
,
i_{j}
is an
R
-vertex of
w_{0}\beta_{i_{1}}\dotsb\beta_{i_{j-1}}
while
i_{j}+1
is an
L
-vertex.

In short, in the drawing of the
(n+r,n)
-web
\varphi(v_{t})
, as one reads the diagram from top-to-bottom each crossing consists of an edge connected to an
R
-vertex passing under an edge connected to a
L
-vertex. If we were to label the vertices by their type, then locally each crossing would look like
